scheduling and missed follow‑through that she initially blamed on \"getting old.\" After a near‑miss where she confused her husband’s medication timing and a friend at church gently pointed out similar patterns in her volunteer work, her primary care physician referred her for a psychiatric evaluation to sort out whether this was early dementia or a longstanding attention problem becoming more visible.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things—calls I promised to make, appointments I just wrote down, even simple errands—and it’s starting to cause problems with my husband’s care.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S005 (Organization_Difficulty): These days when I sit down to sort out my husband’s appointments, my volunteering, and our bills, I just end up with little piles of papers and half‑filled calendars all over the dining table, and I can’t seem to decide what to tackle first so everything gets jumbled and rushed at the last minute.\n- S006 (Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance): If I know I have to sit and really concentrate on something—like filling out his insurance forms online or reading through discharge instructions—I’ll keep finding excuses to put it off, because my brain feels tired before I even start and I dread how quickly I’ll lose track of what I’m doing.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly misplacing things I thought about something I need from the store—pulls my mind away, and I realize I’ve missed what the person just said or lost my place on the page.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Over the past year I keep forgetting everyday things I’ve promised to do—calling the cardiologist back, picking up a prescription on the way home, even simple chores like putting the laundry in the dryer—so I end up apologizing a lot and double‑checking with people because I don’t trust my memory anymore.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): I’ve noticed that when I’m sitting through a long church meeting or waiting with my husband at the clinic, I’m always tapping my foot, rubbing my hands together, or shifting around in the chair without really meaning to, like I just can’t get fully comfortable staying still.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): During longer events, like Bible study or my husband’s appointments, I find myself getting up to straighten the coffee table, use the restroom, or “check on something,” because sitting in one spot for too long makes me restless and antsy even though I know I’m supposed to stay put.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in grade school, my backpack.\n- functional_impairment: Because I keep losing track of appointments and mixing up times or follow-up calls, my husband has almost missed important tests, and I’ve pulled back from volunteering at the senior center and church because I’m afraid I’ll let people down or make a serious mistake.\n- duration: These problems have really stood out and interfered with my day-to-day life I just feel incredibly overwhelmed, stressed, and anxious that I'm going to make a serious mistake with my husband's care.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble with appointments, are you still able to handle your own daily tasks—like managing your medications, preparing meals, and personal care—completely on your own?\n\nPatient: I can still handle basic personal care like getting dressed, but managing the daily household tasks has become quite difficult. I find myself getting distracted while cooking and losing my place in recipes, constantly misplacing my own pill organizer, and forgetting simple chores like moving the laundry to the dryer.\n\nDoctor: Even though these household tasks and managing your medication have become difficult, are you still able to complete them on your own without needing help from others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am still trying to handle all of these tasks on my own because I'm my husband's primary caregiver, but I feel like I'm barely keeping my head above water. I haven't asked anyone for help yet, but I've had to pull back from my volunteering and church activities just to try to keep up. I'm constantly terrified that I'm going to make a serious mistake, especially after I almost mixed up my husband's medication timing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your memory and managing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: While I've had minor habits like this my whole life, these difficulties have really stood out and interfered with my daily life for about a year and a quarter. It became a real problem when my husband's health declined and I had to take on his caregiving and appointment scheduling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any physical medical conditions, or have you experienced any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries. When my primary care doctor referred me here, she didn't mention any physical medical conditions that could be causing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your finances, such as paying bills or keeping track of your budget, and are you still able to do this on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes, managing the bills has become very difficult for me lately. When I sit down to handle them, I just end up with piles of papers and half-filled calendars all over the dining table, and I can't seem to decide what to tackle first. It makes everything feel jumbled and rushed at the last minute, though I am still trying to do it myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding your way around familiar places, or have you experienced times where you felt confused about the date, the year, or where you were?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my way around familiar places, and I've never felt confused about the date, year, or where I am. My issues are really just about keeping track of my daily tasks and paying attention, rather than being disoriented like that.\n\nDoctor: While you are trying to manage everything yourself, have there been actual instances where bills went unpaid, medications were missed, or someone had to step in to help you correct a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, and it's so embarrassing to admit, but I had a scary near-miss where I almost mixed up the timing of my husband’s medications, and a friend at church recently had to gently point out mistakes I was making with our volunteer work. As for the bills, I haven't actually missed a payment yet, but they get so piled up and jumbled on the table that I'm always rushing at the very last second to pay them."

As for the bills, I haven't actually missed a payment yet, but they get so piled up and jumbled on the table that I'm always rushing at the very last second to pay them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to completely hand over any of your daily responsibilities—like managing finances, driving, or organizing medications—to someone else because you could no longer do them safely?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't handed over any of those responsibilities to anyone else yet, and I'm still the one trying to manage my husband's medications, appointments, and our household. However, after that scary near-miss with his medicine timing, I did decide to completely pull back from my volunteering at the church and senior center because I'm just so afraid of making a serious mistake."

I just feel incredibly overwhelmed, stressed, and anxious that I'm going to make a serious mistake with my husband's care.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble with appointments, are you still able to handle your own daily tasks—like managing your medications, preparing meals, and personal care—completely on your own?\n\nPatient: I can still handle basic personal care like getting dressed, but managing the daily household tasks has become quite difficult. I find myself getting distracted while cooking and losing my place in recipes, constantly misplacing my own pill organizer, and forgetting simple chores like moving the laundry to the dryer.\n\nDoctor: Even though these household tasks and managing your medication have become difficult, are you still able to complete them on your own without needing help from others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am still trying to handle all of these tasks on my own because I'm my husband's primary caregiver, but I feel like I'm barely keeping my head above water. I haven't asked anyone for help yet, but I've had to pull back from my volunteering and church activities just to try to keep up. I'm constantly terrified that I'm going to make a serious mistake, especially after I almost mixed up my husband's medication timing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your memory and managing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: While I've had minor habits like this my whole life, these difficulties have really stood out and interfered with my daily life for about a year and a quarter. It became a real problem when my husband's health declined and I had to take on his caregiving and appointment scheduling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any physical medical conditions, or have you experienced any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries. When my primary care doctor referred me here, she didn't mention any physical medical conditions that could be causing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your finances, such as paying bills or keeping track of your budget, and are you still able to do this on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes, managing the bills has become very difficult for me lately. When I sit down to handle them, I just end up with piles of papers and half-filled calendars all over the dining table, and I can't seem to decide what to tackle first. It makes everything feel jumbled and rushed at the last minute, though I am still trying to do it myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding your way around familiar places, or have you experienced times where you felt confused about the date, the year, or where you were?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my way around familiar places, and I've never felt confused about the date, year, or where I am. My issues are really just about keeping track of my daily tasks and paying attention, rather than being disoriented like that.\n\nDoctor: While you are trying to manage everything yourself, have there been actual instances where bills went unpaid, medications were missed, or someone had to step in to help you correct a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, and it's so embarrassing to admit, but I had a scary near-miss where I almost mixed up the timing of my husband’s medications, and a friend at church recently had to gently point out mistakes I was making with our volunteer work. As for the bills, I haven't actually missed a payment yet, but they get so piled up and jumbled on the table that I'm always rushing at the very last second to pay them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to completely hand over any of your daily responsibilities—like managing finances, driving, or organizing medications—to someone else because you could no longer do them safely?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't handed over any of those responsibilities to anyone else yet, and I'm still the one trying to manage my husband's medications, appointments, and our household. However, after that scary near-miss with his medicine timing, I did decide to completely pull back from my volunteering at the church and senior center because I'm just so afraid of making a serious mistake.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor run any specific tests, such as blood work or a brain scan like an MRI or CT, and if so, what were the results?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my primary care doctor doing any brain scans like an MRI or CT, or any specific blood tests for this. She mostly just referred me directly for this evaluation to figure out what was causing my memory and attention issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any chronic medical conditions that you take daily medication for, such as high blood pressure, diabetes, thyroid issues, or high cholesterol?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major chronic medical conditions like high blood pressure or diabetes. I also don't take any daily prescription medications myself."

I find myself getting distracted while cooking and losing my place in recipes, constantly misplacing my own pill organizer, and forgetting simple chores like moving the laundry to the dryer.\n\nDoctor: Even though these household tasks and managing your medication have become difficult, are you still able to complete them on your own without needing help from others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am still trying to handle all of these tasks on my own because I'm my husband's primary caregiver, but I feel like I'm barely keeping my head above water. I haven't asked anyone for help yet, but I've had to pull back from my volunteering and church activities just to try to keep up. I'm constantly terrified that I'm going to make a serious mistake, especially after I almost mixed up my husband's medication timing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your memory and managing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: While I've had minor habits like this my whole life, these difficulties have really stood out and interfered with my daily life for about a year and a quarter. It became a real problem when my husband's health declined and I had to take on his caregiving and appointment scheduling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any physical medical conditions, or have you experienced any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries. When my primary care doctor referred me here, she didn't mention any physical medical conditions that could be causing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your finances, such as paying bills or keeping track of your budget, and are you still able to do this on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes, managing the bills has become very difficult for me lately. When I sit down to handle them, I just end up with piles of papers and half-filled calendars all over the dining table, and I can't seem to decide what to tackle first. It makes everything feel jumbled and rushed at the last minute, though I am still trying to do it myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding your way around familiar places, or have you experienced times where you felt confused about the date, the year, or where you were?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my way around familiar places, and I've never felt confused about the date, year, or where I am. My issues are really just about keeping track of my daily tasks and paying attention, rather than being disoriented like that.\n\nDoctor: While you are trying to manage everything yourself, have there been actual instances where bills went unpaid, medications were missed, or someone had to step in to help you correct a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, and it's so embarrassing to admit, but I had a scary near-miss where I almost mixed up the timing of my husband’s medications, and a friend at church recently had to gently point out mistakes I was making with our volunteer work. As for the bills, I haven't actually missed a payment yet, but they get so piled up and jumbled on the table that I'm always rushing at the very last second to pay them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to completely hand over any of your daily responsibilities—like managing finances, driving, or organizing medications—to someone else because you could no longer do them safely?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't handed over any of those responsibilities to anyone else yet, and I'm still the one trying to manage my husband's medications, appointments, and our household. However, after that scary near-miss with his medicine timing, I did decide to completely pull back from my volunteering at the church and senior center because I'm just so afraid of making a serious mistake.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor run any specific tests, such as blood work or a brain scan like an MRI or CT, and if so, what were the results?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my primary care doctor doing any brain scans like an MRI or CT, or any specific blood tests for this. She mostly just referred me directly for this evaluation to figure out what was causing my memory and attention issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any chronic medical conditions that you take daily medication for, such as high blood pressure, diabetes, thyroid issues, or high cholesterol?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major chronic medical conditions like high blood pressure or diabetes. I also don't take any daily prescription medications myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any changes in your ability to express yourself, find the right words when speaking, or understand what others are saying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any trouble finding the right words or expressing myself, and I can understand others just fine. However, I do sometimes miss what people say simply because I get easily distracted or my mind starts to wander."
  }

I also don't take any daily prescription medications myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any changes in your ability to express yourself, find the right words when speaking, or understand what others are saying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any trouble finding the right words or expressing myself, and I can understand others just fine. However, I do sometimes miss what people say simply because I get easily distracted or my mind starts to wander.\n\nDoctor: When you try to focus on or complete these daily mental tasks, do you experience a strong sense of mental exhaustion or fatigue very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. If I know I have to sit and really concentrate on something, like filling out my husband's online insurance forms, my brain feels tired before I even start. I dread how quickly I'll lose track of what I'm doing, so I find myself making excuses to put those tasks off.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having some minor habits like this your whole life. When you were a child or teenager, did you struggle with paying attention in school, organizing your work, or constantly misplacing things?\n\nPatient: Yes, these difficulties actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl but always daydreaming and losing track of instructions, and I'd constantly forget to turn in my homework unless my mother stood over me and checked my backpack."

I haven't asked anyone for help yet, but I've had to pull back from my volunteering and church activities just to try to keep up. I'm constantly terrified that I'm going to make a serious mistake, especially after I almost mixed up my husband's medication timing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these difficulties with your memory and managing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: While I've had minor habits like this my whole life, these difficulties have really stood out and interfered with my daily life for about a year and a quarter. It became a real problem when my husband's health declined and I had to take on his caregiving and appointment scheduling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any physical medical conditions, or have you experienced any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any recent illnesses, infections, or head injuries. When my primary care doctor referred me here, she didn't mention any physical medical conditions that could be causing this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty managing your finances, such as paying bills or keeping track of your budget, and are you still able to do this on your own?\n\nPatient: Yes, managing the bills has become very difficult for me lately. When I sit down to handle them, I just end up with piles of papers and half-filled calendars all over the dining table, and I can't seem to decide what to tackle first. It makes everything feel jumbled and rushed at the last minute, though I am still trying to do it myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding your way around familiar places, or have you experienced times where you felt confused about the date, the year, or where you were?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my way around familiar places, and I've never felt confused about the date, year, or where I am. My issues are really just about keeping track of my daily tasks and paying attention, rather than being disoriented like that.\n\nDoctor: While you are trying to manage everything yourself, have there been actual instances where bills went unpaid, medications were missed, or someone had to step in to help you correct a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, and it's so embarrassing to admit, but I had a scary near-miss where I almost mixed up the timing of my husband’s medications, and a friend at church recently had to gently point out mistakes I was making with our volunteer work. As for the bills, I haven't actually missed a payment yet, but they get so piled up and jumbled on the table that I'm always rushing at the very last second to pay them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to completely hand over any of your daily responsibilities—like managing finances, driving, or organizing medications—to someone else because you could no longer do them safely?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't handed over any of those responsibilities to anyone else yet, and I'm still the one trying to manage my husband's medications, appointments, and our household. However, after that scary near-miss with his medicine timing, I did decide to completely pull back from my volunteering at the church and senior center because I'm just so afraid of making a serious mistake.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor run any specific tests, such as blood work or a brain scan like an MRI or CT, and if so, what were the results?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my primary care doctor doing any brain scans like an MRI or CT, or any specific blood tests for this. She mostly just referred me directly for this evaluation to figure out what was causing my memory and attention issues.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any chronic medical conditions that you take daily medication for, such as high blood pressure, diabetes, thyroid issues, or high cholesterol?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major chronic medical conditions like high blood pressure or diabetes. I also don't take any daily prescription medications myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any changes in your ability to express yourself, find the right words when speaking, or understand what others are saying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any trouble finding the right words or expressing myself, and I can understand others just fine. However, I do sometimes miss what people say simply because I get easily distracted or my mind starts to wander.\n\nDoctor: When you try to focus on or complete these daily mental tasks, do you experience a strong sense of mental exhaustion or fatigue very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. If I know I have to sit and really concentrate on something, like filling out my husband's online insurance forms, my brain feels tired before I even start. I dread how quickly I'll lose track of what I'm doing, so I find myself making excuses to put those tasks off.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having some minor habits like this your whole life. When you were a child or teenager, did you struggle with paying attention in school, organizing your work, or constantly misplacing things?\n\nPatient: Yes, these difficulties actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright girl but always daydreaming and losing track of instructions, and I'd constantly forget to turn in my homework unless my mother stood over me and checked my backpack.\n\nDoctor: Along with the daydreaming and focus issues, did you also struggle with feeling restless, constantly on the go, or acting impulsively when you were younger?\n\nPatient: No, I don't recall struggling with restlessness, being constantly on the go, or acting impulsively when I was younger. My difficulties back then were almost entirely about daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to turn in my homework, which my teachers often noted on my report cards."

Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.
